For advanced features, JavaScript libraries, and detailed examples, see reference.md. If you need to fill out a PDF form, read forms.md and follow its instructions.\n\n## Quick Start\n\n```python\nfrom pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter\n\n# Read a PDF\nreader = PdfReader(\"document.pdf\")\nprint(f\"Pages: {len(reader.pages)}\")\n\n# Extract text\ntext = \"\"\nfor page in reader.pages:\n    text += page.extract_text()\n```\n\n## Python Libraries\n\n### pypdf - Basic Operations\n\n#### Merge PDFs\n```python\nfrom pypdf import PdfWriter, PdfReader\n\nwriter = PdfWriter()\nfor pdf_file in [\"doc1.pdf\", \"doc2.pdf\", \"doc3.pdf\"]:\n    reader = PdfReader(pdf_file)\n    for page in reader.pages:\n        writer.add_page(page)\n\nwith open(\"merged.pdf\", \"wb\") as output:\n    writer.write(output)\n```\n\n#### Split PDF\n```python\nreader = PdfReader(\"input.pdf\")\nfor i, page in enumerate(reader.pages):\n    writer = PdfWriter()\n    writer.add_page(page)\n    with open(f\"page_{i+1}.pdf\", \"wb\") as output:\n        writer.write(output)\n```\n\n#### Extract Metadata\n```python\nreader = PdfReader(\"document.pdf\")\nmeta = reader.metadata\nprint(f\"Title: {meta.title}\")\nprint(f\"Author: {meta.author}\")\nprint(f\"Subject: {meta.subject}\")\nprint(f\"Creator: {meta.creator}\")\n```\n\n#### Rotate Pages\n```python\nreader = PdfReader(\"input.pdf\")\nwriter = PdfWriter()\n\npage = reader.pages[0]\npage.rotate(90)  # Rotate 90 degrees clockwise\nwriter.add_page(page)\n\nwith open(\"rotated.pdf\", \"wb\") as output:\n    writer.write(output)\n```\n\n### pdfplumber - Text and Table Extraction\n\n#### Extract Text with Layout\n```python\nimport pdfplumber\n\nwith pdfplumber.open(\"document.pdf\") as pdf:\n    for page in pdf.pages:\n        text = page.extract_text()\n        print(text)\n```\n\n#### Extract Tables\n```python\nwith pdfplumber.open(\"document.pdf\") as pdf:\n    for i, page in enumerate(pdf.pages):\n        tables = page.extract_tables()\n        for j, table in enumerate(tables):\n            print(f\"Table {j+1} on page {i+1}:\")\n            for row in table:\n                print(row)\n```\n\n#### Advanced Table Extraction\n```python\nimport pandas as pd\n\nwith pdfplumber.open(\"document.pdf\") as pdf:\n    all_tables = []\n    for page in pdf.pages:\n        tables = page.extract_tables()\n        for table in tables:\n            if table:  # Check if table is not empty\n                df = pd.DataFrame(table[1:], columns=table[0])\n                all_tables.append(df)\n\n# Combine all tables\nif all_tables:\n    combined_df = pd.concat(all_tables, ignore_index=True)\n    combined_df.to_excel(\"extracted_tables.xlsx\", index=False)\n```\n\n### reportlab - Create PDFs\n\n#### Basic PDF Creation\n```python\nfrom reportlab.lib.pagesizes import letter\nfrom reportlab.pdfgen import canvas\n\nc = canvas.Canvas(\"hello.pdf\", pagesize=letter)\nwidth, height = letter\n\n# Add text\nc.drawString(100, height - 100, \"Hello World!\")\nc.drawString(100, height - 120, \"This is a PDF created with reportlab\")\n\n# Add a line\nc.line(100, height - 140, 400, height - 140)\n\n# Save\nc.save()\n```\n\n#### Create PDF with Multiple Pages\n```python\nfrom reportlab.lib.pagesizes import letter\nfrom reportlab.platypus import SimpleDocTemplate, Paragraph, Spacer, PageBreak\nfrom reportlab.lib.styles import getSampleStyleSheet\n\ndoc = SimpleDocTemplate(\"report.pdf\", pagesize=letter)\nstyles = getSampleStyleSheet()\nstory = []\n\n# Add content\ntitle = Paragraph(\"Report Title\", styles['Title'])\nstory.append(title)\nstory.append(Spacer(1, 12))\n\nbody = Paragraph(\"This is the body of the report. \" * 20, styles['Normal'])\nstory.append(body)\nstory.append(PageBreak())\n\n# Page 2\nstory.append(Paragraph(\"Page 2\", styles['Heading1']))\nstory.append(Paragraph(\"Content for page 2\", styles['Normal']))\n\n# Build PDF\ndoc.build(story)\n```\n\n## Command-Line Tools\n\n### pdftotext (poppler-utils)\n```bash\n# Extract text\npdftotext input.pdf output.txt\n\n# Extract text preserving layout\npdftotext -layout input.pdf output.txt\n\n# Extract specific pages\npdftotext -f 1 -l 5 input.pdf output.txt  # Pages 1-5\n```\n\n### qpdf\n```bash\n# Merge PDFs\nqpdf --empty --pages file1.pdf file2.pdf -- merged.pdf\n\n# Split pages\nqpdf input.pdf --pages . 1-5 -- pages1-5.pdf\nqpdf input.pdf --pages . 6-10 -- pages6-10.pdf\n\n# Rotate pages\nqpdf input.pdf output.pdf --rotate=+90:1  # Rotate page 1 by 90 degrees\n\n# Remove password\nqpdf --password=mypassword --decrypt encrypted.pdf decrypted.pdf\n```\n\n### pdftk (if available)\n```bash\n# Merge\npdftk file1.pdf file2.pdf cat output merged.pdf\n\n# Split\npdftk input.pdf burst\n\n# Rotate\npdftk input.pdf rotate 1east output rotated.pdf\n```\n\n## Common Tasks\n\n### Extract Text from Scanned PDFs\n```python\n# Requires: pip install pytesseract pdf2image\nimport pytesseract\nfrom pdf2image import convert_from_path\n\n# Convert PDF to images\nimages = convert_from_path('scanned.pdf')\n\n# OCR each page\ntext = \"\"\nfor i, image in enumerate(images):\n    text += f\"Page {i+1}:\\n\"\n    text += pytesseract.image_to_string(image)\n    text += \"\\n\\n\"\n\nprint(text)\n```\n\n### Add Watermark\n```python\nfrom pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter\n\n# Create watermark (or load existing)\nwatermark = PdfReader(\"watermark.pdf\").pages[0]\n\n# Apply to all pages\nreader = PdfReader(\"document.pdf\")\nwriter = PdfWriter()\n\nfor page in reader.pages:\n    page.merge_page(watermark)\n    writer.add_page(page)\n\nwith open(\"watermarked.pdf\", \"wb\") as output:\n    writer.write(output)\n```\n\n### Extract Images\n```bash\n# Using pdfimages (poppler-utils)\npdfimages -j input.pdf output_prefix\n\n# This extracts all images as output_prefix-000.jpg, output_prefix-001.jpg, etc.\n```\n\n### Password Protection\n```python\nfrom pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter\n\nreader = PdfReader(\"input.pdf\")\nwriter = PdfWriter()\n\nfor page in reader.pages:\n    writer.add_page(page)\n\n# Add password\nwriter.encrypt(\"userpassword\", \"ownerpassword\")\n\nwith open(\"encrypted.pdf\", \"wb\") as output:\n    writer.write(output)\n```\n\n## Quick Reference\n\n| Task | Best Tool | Command/Code Liu1 , K.L. Wang1,2 , and M. Feng3 ∗\n\n1\n\narXiv:0704.0117v1 [quant-ph] 2 Apr 2007\n\n2\n\nThe School of Science, Southwest University of Science and Technology, Mianyang 621010, China\nThe Department of Modern Physics, University of Science and Technology of China, Hefei 230026, China\n3\nState Key Laboratory of Magnetic Resonance and Atomic and Molecular Physics,\nWuhan Institute of Physics and Mathematics, Chinese Academy of Sciences, Wuhan, 430071, China\n(Dated: November 4, 2018)\nWe consider a single ion confined in a trap under radiation of two traveling waves of lasers. In\nthe strong-excitation regime and without the restriction of Lamb-Dicke limit, the Hamiltonian of\nthe system is similar to a driving Jaynes-Cummings model without rotating wave approximation\n(RWA). The approach we developed enables us to present a complete eigensolutions, which makes\nit available to compare with the solutions under the RWA. We find that, the ground state in our\nnon-RWA solution is energically lower than the counterpart under the RWA. If we have the ion in the\nground state, it is equivalent to a spin dependent force on the trapped ion. Discussion is made for\nthe difference between the solutions with and without the RWA, and for the relevant experimental\ntest, as well as for the possible application in quantum information processing.\nPACS numbers: 32.80.Lg, 42.50.-p, 03.67.-a\n\nI.\n\nINTRODUCTION\n\nUltracold ions trapped as a line are considered as a promising system for quantum information processing [1]. Since\nthe first quantum gate performed in the ion trap [2], there have been a series of experiments with trapped ions to\nachieve nonclassical states [3], simple quantum algorithm [4], and quantum communication [5].\nThere have been also a number of proposals to employ trapped ions for quantum computing, most of which work\nonly in the weak excitation regime (WER), i.e., the Rabi frequency smaller than the trap frequency. While as bigger\nRabi frequency would lead to faster quantum gating, some proposals [6, 7, 8] have aimed to achieve operations in\nthe case of the Rabi frequency larger than the trap frequency, i.e., the so called strong excitation regime (SER). The\ndifference of the WER from the SER is mathematically reflected in the employment of the rotating wave approximation\n(RWA), which averages out the fast oscillating terms in the interaction Hamiltonian. As the RWA is less valid with\nthe larger Rabi frequency, the treatment for the SER was complicated, imcomplete [9], and sometimes resorted to\nnumerics [10].\nIn addition, the Lamb-Dicke limit strongly restricts the application of the trapped ions due to technical challenge\nand the slow quantum gating. We have noticed some ideas [11, 12] to remove the Lamb-Dicke limit in designing\nquantum gates, which are achieved by using some complicated laser pulse sequences.\nIn the present work, we investigate, from another research angle, the system mentioned above in SER and in the\nabsence of the Lamb-Dicke limit. The main idea, based on an analytical approach we have developed, is to check the\neigenvectors and the eigenenergies of such a system, with which we hope to obtain new insight into the system for\nmore application. The main result in our work is a newly found ground state, energically lower than the ground state\ncalculated by standard Jaynes-Cummings model. Eschner‡\nFirst order Doppler effects are usually ignored in laser driven trapped ions when the recoil frequency is much smaller than the trapping frequency (Lamb-Dicke regime). This means that the\ncentral, carrier excitation band is supposed to be unaffected by vibronic transitions in which the\nvibrational number changes. While this is strictly true in the Lamb-Dicke limit (infinitely tight\nconfinement), the vibronic transitions do play a role in the Lamb-Dicke regime. In this paper we\nquantify the asymptotic behaviour of their effect with respect to the Lamb-Dicke parameter. In\nparticular, we give analytical expressions for the frequency shift, “pulling” or “pushing”, produced\nin the carrier absorption band by the vibronic transitions both for Rabi and Ramsey schemes. The redundant\n1D diffusion near the specific binding site extends the target size and facilitates target\nlocation. While this model successfully predicts the kinetics measured in test tubes, it has not\nbeen extended to account for the highly crowded environment in living cells. Here, we\ninvestigate the effect of other DNA-binding proteins that partially occupy the bacterial\nchromosome. We show how they would slow down the search process, mainly through\nrestricted sliding near the target. This implies that increasing the overall DNA-binding\nprotein concentration would have a marginal effect in reducing the search time, because any\nadditional proteins would restrict the search process for each other. While the presence of\nother proteins prevents sliding from the flanking DNA, DNA looping provides an\nalternative path to transfer from neighboring sites to the target efficiently. We propose that,\nwhen looping is faster than the initial search process, the auxiliary binding sites further\nextend the effective target region and therefore facilitate the target location.\n\nIntroduction\n\nDNA-binding proteins control both when and how the genome functions. While this model successfully predicts the kinetics measured in test tubes, it has not\nbeen extended to account for the highly crowded environment in living cells. Here, we\ninvestigate the effect of other DNA-binding proteins that partially occupy the bacterial\nchromosome. We show how they would slow down the search process, mainly through\nrestricted sliding near the target. This implies that increasing the overall DNA-binding\nprotein concentration would have a marginal effect in reducing the search time, because any\nadditional proteins would restrict the search process for each other. While the presence of\nother proteins prevents sliding from the flanking DNA, DNA looping provides an\nalternative path to transfer from neighboring sites to the target efficiently. We propose that,\nwhen looping is faster than the initial search process, the auxiliary binding sites further\nextend the effective target region and therefore facilitate the target location.\n\nIntroduction\n\nDNA-binding proteins control both when and how the genome functions. Moreover, they limit how fast\nthe genomic information can be accessed. For instance, the rate at which a transcription factor (TF) finds its\nspecific binding site determines how rapidly the corresponding gene can be turned on or off. When this rate is","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false}}

R. China\n\nAbstract\nIn the standard viewpoint, the temperature of a stationary black hole is proportional\nto its surface gravity, TH = h̄κ/2π. This is a semiclassical result and the quantum gravity\neffects are not taken into consideration. The\ninterplay between strong covalent bonds of the backbone\nand weak hydrogen interactions between bases [1], the\nthermal bath in which it is immersed, and the proximity\nof physiological conditions to the denaturation temperature, make DNA highly and non-linearly responsive to\nmechanical and thermal changes, and render any solely\nmechanical approach inviable. This is critical for nanotechnology, where DNA is the basis for novel materials [2], but understanding double helix thermomechanics\nwould also illuminate biology, where enzymes involved in\nreplication and repair are viewed as molecular motors.\nIn the past twenty years, direct single molecule manipulation [3, 4] has revolutionized our understanding of key\naspects of DNA, revealing new couplings and transitions\nbetween different structures, whose nature and forms,\nhowever, are still speculative. We propose a methodology to study music development by\napplying multivariate statistics on composers characteristics. Seven representative composers were considered\nin terms of eight main musical features. Grades were assigned to each characteristic and their correlations\nwere analyzed. A bootstrap method was applied to simulate hundreds of artificial composers influenced by the\nseven representatives chosen. Applying dimensionality reduction we obtained a planar space used to quantify\nnon-numeric relations like dialectics, opposition and innovation. Composers differences on style and technique\nwere represented as geometrical distances in the planar space, making it possible to quantify, for example,\nhow much Bach and Stockhausen differ from other composers or how much Beethoven influenced Brahms.\nIn addition, we compared the results with a prior investigation on philosophy1 . The influence of dialectics,\nstrong on philosophy, was not remarkable on music. Instead, supporting an observation already considered\nby music theorists, strong influences were identified between subsequent composers, implying inheritance and\nsuggesting a stronger master-disciple evolution when compared to the philosophy analysis.\nPACS numbers: 89.75.Fb,05.65.+b\nKeywords: music, musicology, pattern recognition, statistics\nI.\n\nINTRODUCTION\n\nAlong the history of music, composers developed their\nown styles along a continuous search for coherence or\n\n=== 1202.2518v4.pdf ===\nSegmenting DNA sequence into ‘words’\nWang Liang, Zhao KaiYong\nTencent, SOSO, Beijing, P.R. But for some languages like Chinese,\nthere is no space between letters. The fact that there are no delimiters in sequence posed well\nknow problem of word segmentation. So if we\ncould segment DNA sequence into DNA “words” sequence, we could apply many mature\ninformation processing technologies to study DNA sequences. However, from the point of view of General Relativity, if the particle’s\nenergy density exceeds a certain threshold, it should be a black hole. In order to combine these two\npictures, we introduce a horizon wave-function determined by the particle wave-function in position\nspace, which eventually yields the probability that the particle is a black hole. The existence of a\nminimum mass for black holes naturally follows, albeit not in the form of a sharp value around the\nPlanck scale, but rather like a vanishing probability that a particle much lighter than the Planck\nmass be a black hole. We also show that our construction entails an effective Generalised Uncertainty\nPrinciple (GUP), simply obtained by adding the uncertainties coming from the two wave-functions\nassociated to a particle. Finally, the decay of microscopic (quantum) black holes is also described\nin agreement with what the GUP predicts.\nPACS numbers: 04.70.Dy,04.70.-s,04.60.-m\n\nI.\n\n\n=== 1308.3843v1.pdf ===\nCoarse-graining DNA for simulations of DNA nanotechnology\nJonathan P. Tests against numerical-relativity results are carried out,\nshowing that both the dimensionless spin parameter and the final mass are accurately reproduced. For the first\ntime, the behavior of both quantities and of the l = 2, m = 2, n = 0 quasinormal mode frequency is inspected\nthroughout the parameter space. Predictions of this frequency may be exploited to guide gravitational-wave\nmodeling and detection efforts and to extract physical information from detected gravitational-wave signals that\nwould help us break degeneracies between binary black hole and black hole-neutron star systems, improve our\nunderstanding of compact binary formation, and constrain the neutron star equation of state.\nPACS numbers: 04.25.dk, 04.30.Db, 95.30.Sf, 97.60.Jd\n\narXiv:1311.5931v2 [gr-qc] 10 Mar 2014\n\nI.\n\nINTRODUCTION\n\nFollowing its formation, a black hole-neutron star (BH-NS)\nbinary system loses orbital energy and angular momentum by\nemitting gravitational radiation. During the inspiral stage, it\nreadjusts its orbital separation accordingly, until the energy\nreservoir is emptied and the two compact objects merge. The\nremnant of this coalescence consists of a black hole possibly\nsurrounded by a hot, massive accretion torus [1]. BH-NS binaries have not been observed yet, but population synthesis\nstudies suggest that BH-NS mergers are likely to occur frequently in the Hubble volume [2–5]. By detecting the radiation emitted during their inspiral and merger, gravitationalwave (GW) observatories — such as LIGO [6], Virgo [7],\nKAGRA [8], and the Einstein Telescope [9] — are therefore likely to be involved in the first direct observations of\nBH-NS systems. We describe an algorithm to detect genomic regularities, within a blind discovery\nstrategy. The algorithm uses information profiles built using suitable combinations of finitecontext models. We used the genome of the fission yeast Schizosaccharomyces pombe strain\n972 h− for illustration, unveiling locations of low information content, which are usually\nassociated with DNA regions of potential biological interest.\n\nIntroduction\nGraphical representations of DNA sequences are a handy way of quickly finding regions of potential interest. This has been a topic addressed using various approaches\n(see, for example, [1–10]), some of them relying on information theoretical principles.\nBoth global and local estimates of the randomness of a sequence provide useful information, but both also have shortcomings. Global estimates do not show how the\ncharacteristics change along the sequence. Local estimates fail to take into consideration the global properties of the sequence. This latter drawback was addressed by\nClift et al. [11] using the concept of sequence landscape, plots displaying the number\nof times oligonucleotides from the target sequence occur in a given source sequence.\nIf the target and source sequences coincide, then the landscape provides information\nabout self-similarities (repeats) of the target sequence.\nThe sequence landscapes of Clift et al. [11] have been a first attempt to display\nlocal information, taking into account global characteristics of the sequence. This\nidea was pursed by Allison et al. The black hole\nentropy law inspired the formulation of the holographic principle by ’t Hooft and\nSusskind, which is famously realized in the gauge/gravity correspondence by Maldacena, Gubser–Klebanov–Polaykov and Witten within string theory. Moreover, the\nmicroscopic derivation of black hole entropy, pioneered by Strominger–Vafa within\nstring theory, often serves as a consistency check for putative theories of quantum\ngravity. In this book chapter we review these developments over five decades, starting in the 1960ies.\n\nIntroduction and Prehistory\nIntroductory remarks. The history of black hole thermodynamics is intertwined\nwith the history of quantum gravity. In the absence of experimental data capable of\nprobing Planck scale physics the best we can do is to subject putative theories of\nquantum gravity to stringent consistency checks. Black hole thermodynamics provides a number of highly non-trivial consistency checks. We present a novel alignment-free computational\nmethod for sequence analysis based on Ramanujan-Fourier transform (RFT),\nin which complete information of DNA sequences is retained. We represent\nDNA sequences as four binary indicator sequences and apply RFT on the\nindicator sequences to convert them into frequency domain. The Euclidean\ndistance of the complete RFT coeﬃcients of DNA sequences are used as\nsimilarity measure. To address the diﬀerent lengths in Euclidean space of\nRFT coeﬃcients, we pad zeros to short DNA binary sequences so that the\nbinary sequences equal the longest length in the comparison sequence data.\nThus, the DNA sequences are compared in the same dimensional frequency\nspace without information loss. We demonstrate the usefulness of the proposed method by presenting experimental results on hierarchical clustering of\ngenes and genomes. As case studies, we analyzed the\ntemporal evolution of classical music and philosophy by obtaining data for 8 features\ncharacterizing the corresponding fields for 7 well-known composers and philosophers,\nwhich were treated with multivariate statistics and pattern recognition methods. A\nbootstrap method was applied to avoid statistical bias caused by the small sample\ndata set, with which hundreds of artificial composers and philosophers were generated,\ninfluenced by the 7 names originally chosen. This leads\nto an understanding of black holes from the viewpoint of chemistry, in terms of concepts such\nas Van der Waals fluids, reentrant phase transitions, and triple points. Gupta\nDhirubhai Ambani Institute of Information and Communication Technology\nGandhinagar, Gujarat, 382007 India\nEmail: dlimbachiya@acm.org and mankg@computer.org\n\nAbstract\n\narXiv:1501.07133v2 [cs.IT] 22 Apr 2016\n\n1\n\nDNA based storage systems received attention by many researchers. This includes archival and re-writable random access\nDNA based storage systems. In this work, we have developed an efficient technique to encode the data into DNA sequence by\nusing non-linear families of ternary codes. In particular, we proposes an algorithm to encode data into DNA with high information\nstorage density and better error correction using a sub code of Golay code. Theoretically, 115 exabytes (EB) data can be stored\nin one gram of DNA by our method.\n\nI. Thus it\nis natural to expect that coding theory will be the foundational subject for the DNA computing paradigm. For the successful\nexperiments with DNA computing usually we design DNA strings which are sufficiently dissimilar. We extend a recently\ndeveloped test charge theory [S. Buyukdagli et al., Phys. Rev. E 94, 042502 (2016)] to the case of\na stiff polymer interacting with a DNA molecule in an electrolyte mixture. The theory accounts for\none-loop level electrostatic correlation effects such as the ionic cloud deformation around the strongly\ncharged DNA molecule as well as image-charge forces induced by the low DNA permittivity. Our\nmodel can reproduce and explain various characteristics of the experimental phase diagrams for\npolymer solutions. First, the addition of polyvalent cations to the electrolyte solution results in\nthe attraction of the negatively charged polymer by the DNA molecule. The glue of the like-charge\nattraction is the enhanced shielding of the polymer charges by the dense counterion layer at the\nDNA surface. Secondly, through the shielding of the DNA-induced electrostatic potential, mono- and\npolyvalent cations of large concentration both suppress the like-charge attraction. Within the same\nformalism, we also predict a new opposite-charge repulsion effect between the DNA molecule and a\npositively charged polymer. In the presence of polyvalent anions such as sulfate or phosphate, their\nrepulsion by the DNA charges leads to the charge screening deficiency of the region around the DNA\nmolecule. This translates into a repulsive force that results in the decomplexation of the polymer\nfrom DNA. This opposite-charge repulsion phenomenon can be verified by current experiments and\nthe underlying mechanism can be beneficial to gene therapeutic applications where the control over\npolymer-DNA interactions is the key factor.\nPACS numbers: 05.20.Jj,82.45.Gj,82.35.Rs\n\nI.\n\n\n=== 1708.07404v3.pdf ===\nMass of Schwarzschild Black Holes Is Indeed Zero And Black Hole Candidates Are\nQuasi Black Holes\nAbhas Mitra∗\n\narXiv:1708.07404v3 [physics.gen-ph] 10 May 2018\n\nHomi Bhabha National Institute, Mumbai-400094, India\n(Dated: May 11, 2018)\nA Schwarzschild Black Hole (BH) is the gravitational field due to a neutral point mass, and it\nturns out that the gravitational mass of a neutral point mass: M = 0 (Arnowitt, Deser, Misner, PRL\n4, 375, 1960). The same result is also suggested by Janis, Newman, and Winicour (PRL 20, 878,\n1968). In 1969, Bel gave an explicit proof that for a Schwarzschild BH, M = 0 (Bel, JMP 10, 1051,\n1969). The same result follows from the fact the timelike geodesic of a test particle would turn null\nif it would ever arrive at an event horizon (Mitra, FPL, 2000, 2002). Non-occurrrence of trapped\nsurfaces in continued gravitational collapse too demands M = 0 for black hole (Mitra, Pramana,\n73, 615, 2009). Physically, for a point mass at R = 0, one expects Ric ∼ M δ(R = 0) (Narlikar &\nPadmanabhan, Found. Phys., 18, 659, 1988). But the black hole solution is obtained from Ric = 0.\nAgain this is the most direct proof that M = 0 for a Schwarzschild black hole. Implication of\nthis result is that the observed massive black hole candidates are non-singular quasi black holes or\nblack hole mimickers which can possess strong magnetic fields as has been observed. We extend a recently\ndeveloped test charge theory [S. Buyukdagli et al., Phys. Rev. E 94, 042502 (2016)] to the case of\na stiff polymer interacting with a DNA molecule in an electrolyte mixture. The theory accounts for\none-loop level electrostatic correlation effects such as the ionic cloud deformation around the strongly\ncharged DNA molecule as well as image-charge forces induced by the low DNA permittivity. Our\nmodel can reproduce and explain various characteristics of the experimental phase diagrams for\npolymer solutions. First, the addition of polyvalent cations to the electrolyte solution results in\nthe attraction of the negatively charged polymer by the DNA molecule. The glue of the like-charge\nattraction is the enhanced shielding of the polymer charges by the dense counterion layer at the\nDNA surface. Secondly, through the shielding of the DNA-induced electrostatic potential, mono- and\npolyvalent cations of large concentration both suppress the like-charge attraction. Within the same\nformalism, we also predict a new opposite-charge repulsion effect between the DNA molecule and a\npositively charged polymer. In the presence of polyvalent anions such as sulfate or phosphate, their\nrepulsion by the DNA charges leads to the charge screening deficiency of the region around the DNA\nmolecule. This translates into a repulsive force that results in the decomplexation of the polymer\nfrom DNA. This opposite-charge repulsion phenomenon can be verified by current experiments and\nthe underlying mechanism can be beneficial to gene therapeutic applications where the control over\npolymer-DNA interactions is the key factor.\nPACS numbers: 05.20.Jj,82.45.Gj,82.35.Rs\n\nI.\n\n\n=== 1708.07404v3.pdf ===\nMass of Schwarzschild Black Holes Is Indeed Zero And Black Hole Candidates Are\nQuasi Black Holes\nAbhas Mitra∗\n\narXiv:1708.07404v3 [physics.gen-ph] 10 May 2018\n\nHomi Bhabha National Institute, Mumbai-400094, India\n(Dated: May 11, 2018)\nA Schwarzschild Black Hole (BH) is the gravitational field due to a neutral point mass, and it\nturns out that the gravitational mass of a neutral point mass: M = 0 (Arnowitt, Deser, Misner, PRL\n4, 375, 1960). The same result is also suggested by Janis, Newman, and Winicour (PRL 20, 878,\n1968). In 1969, Bel gave an explicit proof that for a Schwarzschild BH, M = 0 (Bel, JMP 10, 1051,\n1969). The same result follows from the fact the timelike geodesic of a test particle would turn null\nif it would ever arrive at an event horizon (Mitra, FPL, 2000, 2002). Non-occurrrence of trapped\nsurfaces in continued gravitational collapse too demands M = 0 for black hole (Mitra, Pramana,\n73, 615, 2009). Physically, for a point mass at R = 0, one expects Ric ∼ M δ(R = 0) (Narlikar &\nPadmanabhan, Found. Phys., 18, 659, 1988). But the black hole solution is obtained from Ric = 0.\nAgain this is the most direct proof that M = 0 for a Schwarzschild black hole. Implication of\nthis result is that the observed massive black hole candidates are non-singular quasi black holes or\nblack hole mimickers which can possess strong magnetic fields as has been observed. The echoes\nfrom LIGO signals, if true, may be the direct evidence that the pertinent compact objects are BH\nmimickers and not true vacuum BHs.\nINTRODUCTION\n\nStarting from Einstein, most of the founding fathers of general relativity thought that though the vacuum\nSchwarzschild equation is correct, its interpretation in terms of black holes is incorrect. Essentially, they pleaded\nthat while a massive star can contract almost upto its Schwarzschild surface, it cannot contract upto the redline\nR = Rs = 2M (G = c = 1), and its radius R > 2M . The crux of the problem lies in\nan assumption about the structure of entanglement across the event horizon, namely, that the\nHilbert space factorizes. While valid in quantum mechanics, this fails drastically in quantum\nfield theory, and hence a deeper understanding of entanglement is required if further progress\nis to be made. Recently, ideas from algebraic quantum field theory have provided new insight\ninto this issue, and show promise for elucidating the connection between entropy and horizons\nthat underlies black hole thermodynamics.\n\nEssay written for the Black Hole Initiative’s 2018 paper competition.\n\n\fIn 1973, Jacob Bekenstein observed [1] that black holes must be endowed with an entropy\nin order to preserve the second law of thermodynamics; otherwise, one could decrease the\nentropy of the universe by simply throwing subsystems with high entropy into a black hole.\nAt face value, this is an intuitive proposal: since the information about the degrees of freedom\nthat comprise the hypothetical subsystem would then be hidden behind the event horizon, it\nmakes sense to count them among the microstates of the black hole.\nThe unintuitive twist comes from the realization that this naı̈ve bookkeeping is not at\nall how black holes operate. The entropy of familiar systems scales with the volume thereof,\nwhich is consistent with simply counting the constituent (e.g., particulate) degrees of freedom.\nBlack hole entropy, in stark contrast, scales with the area of the event horizon. The crux of the problem lies in\nan assumption about the structure of entanglement across the event horizon, namely, that the\nHilbert space factorizes. While valid in quantum mechanics, this fails drastically in quantum\nfield theory, and hence a deeper understanding of entanglement is required if further progress\nis to be made. Recently, ideas from algebraic quantum field theory have provided new insight\ninto this issue, and show promise for elucidating the connection between entropy and horizons\nthat underlies black hole thermodynamics.\n\nEssay written for the Black Hole Initiative’s 2018 paper competition.\n\n\fIn 1973, Jacob Bekenstein observed [1] that black holes must be endowed with an entropy\nin order to preserve the second law of thermodynamics; otherwise, one could decrease the\nentropy of the universe by simply throwing subsystems with high entropy into a black hole.\nAt face value, this is an intuitive proposal: since the information about the degrees of freedom\nthat comprise the hypothetical subsystem would then be hidden behind the event horizon, it\nmakes sense to count them among the microstates of the black hole.\nThe unintuitive twist comes from the realization that this naı̈ve bookkeeping is not at\nall how black holes operate. The entropy of familiar systems scales with the volume thereof,\nwhich is consistent with simply counting the constituent (e.g., particulate) degrees of freedom.\nBlack hole entropy, in stark contrast, scales with the area of the event horizon. Almudever, Koen Bertels\nDepartment of Quantum & Computer Engineering\nDelft University of Technology, The Netherlands\nSeptember 2019\nAbstract\nWith small-scale quantum processors transitioning from experimental physics labs to industrial products, these processors allow us to efficiently compute important algorithms in various fields. In this\npaper, we propose a quantum algorithm to address the challenging field of big data processing for\ngenome sequence reconstruction. This research describes an architecture-aware implementation of a\nquantum algorithm for sub-sequence alignment. A new algorithm named QiBAM (quantum indexed\nbidirectional associative memory) is proposed, that uses approximate pattern-matching based on Hamming distances. QiBAM extends the Grover’s search algorithm in two ways to allow for: (1) approximate matches needed for read errors in genomics, and (2) a distributed search for multiple solutions\nover the quantum encoding of DNA sequences. This approach gives a quadratic speedup over the classical algorithm. A full implementation of the algorithm is provided and verified using the OpenQL\ncompiler and QX simulator framework. This represents a first exploration towards a full-stack quantum accelerated genome sequencing pipeline design. The open-source implementation can be found on\nhttps://github.com/prince-ph0en1x/QAGS.\n\nKeywords: quantum algorithms, quantum search, DNA read alignment, genomics, associative memory,\naccelerators, in-memory computing\n\n1\n\nIntroduction\n\nThe idea of using the fundamental physical building blocks of nature for computation, as proposed by\nRichard Feynman [1] in 1960s, laid the foundation for the second quantum revolution, focusing on quantum\ncomputation instead of the physics of quantum mechanics. Scales\nare specific to specific genre of music. In this second article of\nthe series we explore the development of various scales associated with western classical music, arguably the most influential genre of music of the present time.\n\nIntroduction\nJust like an alphabet is the basis of a language, a set of basic\nnotes known as a scale forms the basis of music. The term ‘scale’\ncomes from the Latin word ‘scala’ meaning ‘ladder’. Thus a scale\nis a ladder or a set of notes ordered according to their frequency or\npitch. A scale ordered by increasing pitch is an ascending scale,\nand a scale ordered by decreasing pitch is a descending scale.\nScales, in general, may or may not contain the same pitches while\nascending as well as when descending. Moreover, even all of the\nscale steps may not be equal. Indeed, most of the traditional music scales began with unequal scale steps. Due to the principle\nof octave equivalence, scales are generally considered to span a\nsingle octave, with higher or lower octaves simply repeating the\npattern. A musical scale represents a division of the octave space\ninto a specific number of scale steps, a scale step being the interval between two successive notes of the scale.\n\nSushan Konar works on\nstellar compact objects. In particular, mergers of stellar black holes are the main targets of the current gravitational wave experiments.\nIn order to make accurate predictions, it is however necessary to simulate the mergers in numerical\ngeneral relativity, which requires high performance computing. While scaling relations are used\nto rescale simulations for very massive black holes, primordial black holes have specific properties\nwhich can invalidate the rescaling. Similarly black holes in theories beyond Einstein’s relativity\ncan have different scaling properties. In this article, we consider scaling relations for the most\ngeneral cases of primordial black holes, such as charged and spinned black holes, and study the\neffects of the cosmological expansion and of Hawking evaporation. We also consider more exotic\nblack hole models and derive the corresponding scaling relations, which can be compared to the\nobservations in order to identify the underlying black hole model and can be used to rescale the\nnumerical simulations of exotic black hole mergers.\n\n\n=== 2005.11841v3.pdf ===\nscadnano: A browser-based, scriptable tool for\ndesigning DNA nanostructures\nDavid Doty1\nUniversity of California, Davis, USA\nhttps://web.cs.ucdavis.edu/~doty/\n\ndoty@ucdavis.edu\n\nBenjamin L Lee\nUniversity of California, Davis, USA\nbnllee@ucdavis.edu\n\nTristan Stérin\nMaynooth University\nhttps://dna.hamilton.ie/tsterin/index.html\n\narXiv:2005.11841v3 [cs.ET] 17 Jul 2020\n\nTristan.Sterin@mu.ie\n\nAbstract\nWe introduce scadnano (short for “scriptable cadnano”), a computational tool for designing synthetic\nDNA structures. Savaged\n\narXiv:2011.02460v1 [q-bio.PE] 4 Nov 2020\n\na\n\nDepartment of Psychology, The Graduate Center, City University of New York, New York, NY, USA\nb\nDepartment of Biology, Queens College, City University of New York, Flushing, NY, USA\nc\nDepartment of Philosophy, DCLPS, Heinrich-Heine University, Düsseldorf, NRW, Germany\nd\nFaculty of Environment and Information Studies, Keio University SFC, Fujisawa, Japan\n1\nmasonyoungblood@gmail.com\n\nAbstract\nCultural phylogenies, or “trees” of culture, are typically built using methods from biology that use similarities and\ndifferences in artifacts to infer the historical relationships between the populations that produced them. While these\nmethods have yielded important insights, particularly in linguistics, researchers continue to debate the extent to which\ncultural phylogenies are tree-like or reticulated due to high levels of horizontal transmission. In this study, we propose a\nnovel method for phylogenetic reconstruction using dynamic community detection that explicitly accounts for transmission\nbetween lineages. We used data from 1,498,483 collaborative relationships between electronic music artists to construct\na cultural phylogeny based on observed population structure. The results suggest that, although the phylogeny is fundamentally tree-like, horizontal transmission is common and populations never become fully isolated from one another.\nIn addition, we found evidence that electronic music diversity has increased between 1975 and 1999. The method used\nin this study is available as a new R package called DynCommPhylo. Future studies should apply this method to other\ncultural systems such as academic publishing and film, as well as biological systems where high resolution reproductive\ndata is available, to assess how levels of reticulation in evolution vary across domains.\n\n=== 2012.02117v1.pdf ===\nProbing the Black Hole Metric. In this paper,\nwe quantify the upper bounds on potential black-hole metric deviations imposed by observations of\nblack-hole shadows and of binary black-hole inspirals in order to explore the current experimental\nlimits on possible violations of the last two predictions. We find that both types of experiments\nprovide correlated constraints on deviation parameters that are primarily in the tt-components of the\nspacetimes, when expressed in areal coordinates. We conclude that, currently, there is no evidence\nfor a deviations from the Kerr metric across the 8 orders of magnitudes in masses and 16 orders in\ncurvatures spanned by the two types of black holes. Moreover, because of the particular masses of\nblack holes in the current sample of gravitational-wave sources, the correlations imposed by the two\nexperiments are aligned and of similar magnitudes when expressed in terms of the far field, postNewtonian predictions of the metrics. In this work, we develop a theoretical framework\nthat describes the dynamics of ions in time-varying potentials with a motional squeeze operator,\nfollowed by a motional displacement operator. Using this framework, we develop a new, general\nprotocol for trapped ion transport, separation, and merging. We show that motional squeezing can\nprepare an ion wave packet to enable transfer from the ground state of one trapping potential to\nanother. The framework and protocol are applicable if the potential is harmonic over the extent\nof the ion wave packets at all times. As illustrations, we discuss two specific operations: changing\nthe strength of the confining potential for a single ion, and separating same-species ions with their\nmutual Coulomb force. Both of these operations are, ideally, free of residual motional excitation.\n\nA suitable platform for quantum information processing must enable the precise control of many-body quantum states [1, 2]. Savaged\n\narXiv:2011.02460v1 [q-bio.PE] 4 Nov 2020\n\na\n\nDepartment of Psychology, The Graduate Center, City University of New York, New York, NY, USA\nb\nDepartment of Biology, Queens College, City University of New York, Flushing, NY, USA\nc\nDepartment of Philosophy, DCLPS, Heinrich-Heine University, Düsseldorf, NRW, Germany\nd\nFaculty of Environment and Information Studies, Keio University SFC, Fujisawa, Japan\n1\nmasonyoungblood@gmail.com\n\nAbstract\nCultural phylogenies, or “trees” of culture, are typically built using methods from biology that use similarities and\ndifferences in artifacts to infer the historical relationships between the populations that produced them. While these\nmethods have yielded important insights, particularly in linguistics, researchers continue to debate the extent to which\ncultural phylogenies are tree-like or reticulated due to high levels of horizontal transmission. In this study, we propose a\nnovel method for phylogenetic reconstruction using dynamic community detection that explicitly accounts for transmission\nbetween lineages. We used data from 1,498,483 collaborative relationships between electronic music artists to construct\na cultural phylogeny based on observed population structure. The results suggest that, although the phylogeny is fundamentally tree-like, horizontal transmission is common and populations never become fully isolated from one another.\nIn addition, we found evidence that electronic music diversity has increased between 1975 and 1999. The method used\nin this study is available as a new R package called DynCommPhylo. Future studies should apply this method to other\ncultural systems such as academic publishing and film, as well as biological systems where high resolution reproductive\ndata is available, to assess how levels of reticulation in evolution vary across domains.\n\n=== 2012.02117v1.pdf ===\nProbing the Black Hole Metric. Cai,1, 2, ∗ Y.-K. Wu,1, ∗ Q.-X. Mei,1 W.-D. Zhao,1 Y. Jiang,1 L. Yao,1, 2 L. He,1 Z.-C. Zhou,1, 3 and L.-M. Duan1, †\n\narXiv:2205.14860v1 [quant-ph] 30 May 2022\n\n1\n\nCenter for Quantum Information, Institute for Interdisciplinary Information Sciences,\nTsinghua University, Beijing, 100084, PR China\n2\nHYQ Co., Ltd., Beijing, 100176, PR China\n3\nBeijing Academy of Quantum Information Sciences, Beijing 100193, PR China\n\nSupersymmetry (SUSY) helps solve the hierarchy problem in high-energy physics and provides\na natural groundwork for unifying gravity with other fundamental interactions. While being one\nof the most promising frameworks for theories beyond the Standard Model, its direct experimental evidence in nature still remains to be discovered. Here we report experimental realization of a\nsupersymmetric quantum mechanics (SUSY QM) model, a reduction of the SUSY quantum field\ntheory for studying its fundamental properties, using a trapped ion quantum simulator. We demonstrate the energy degeneracy caused by SUSY in this model and the spontaneous SUSY breaking.\nBy a partial quantum state tomography of the spin-phonon coupled system, we explicitly measure\nthe supercharge of the degenerate ground states, which are superpositions of the bosonic and the\nfermionic states. Our work demonstrates the trapped-ion quantum simulator as an economic yet\npowerful platform to study versatile physics in a single well-controlled system.\n\n\n=== 2206.06546v1.pdf ===\nIndividual addressing of trapped ion qubits with geometric phase gates\nR. T. Sutherland,1, ∗ R. Srinivas,2 and D. T. C. Allcock3, 4\n\narXiv:2206.06546v1 [quant-ph] 14 Jun 2022\n\n1\nDepartment of Electrical and Computer Engineering,\nUniversity of Texas at San Antonio, San Antonio, Texas 78249, USA\n2\nDepartment of Physics, University of Oxford, Clarendon Laboratory, Parks Road, Oxford, OX1 3PU, UK\n3\nOxford Ionics Limited, Begbroke Science Park, Begbroke, OX5 1PF, UK\n4\nDepartment of Physics, University of Oregon, Eugene, Oregon 97403, USA\n(Dated: June 15, 2022)\n\nWe propose a new scheme for individual addressing of trapped ion qubits, selecting them via\ntheir motional frequency. We show that geometric phase gates can perform single-qubit rotations\nusing the coherent interference of spin-independent and (global) spin-dependent forces. The spinindependent forces, which can be generated via localised electric fields, increase the gate speed\nwhile reducing its sensitivity to motional decoherence, which we show analytically and numerically.\nWhile the scheme applies to most trapped ion experimental setups, we numerically simulate a specific\nlaser-free implementation, showing cross-talk errors below 10−6 for reasonable parameters.\n\nTo achieve universal error-corrected quantum computation, we require one- and two-qubit gates with infidelities below ∼ 10−5 and ∼ 10−4 , respectively [1, 2]. Perhaps more challenging, we must also perform them in a\nsystem that can scale to thousands of qubits. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"call_function_i20h8dp34e7u_1","type":"tool_result","content":"=== 2305.12773v1.pdf ===\nHow to wire a 1000-qubit trapped ion quantum computer\nM. Malinowski,1, ∗ D. T. C. Allcock,1, 2 and C. J. Ballance1, 3\n1\nOxford Ionics, Oxford, OX5 1PF, UK\nDepartment of Physics, University of Oregon, Eugene, OR 97403\n3\nDepartment of Physics, University of Oxford, Clarendon Laboratory, Parks Road, Oxford, OX1 3PU, UK\n\narXiv:2305.12773v1 [quant-ph] 22 May 2023\n\n2\n\nOne of the most formidable challenges of scaling up quantum computers is that of control signal\ndelivery. Today’s small-scale quantum computers typically connect each qubit to one or more\nseparate external signal sources. This approach is not scalable due to the I/O limitations of the\nqubit chip, necessitating the integration of control electronics. However, it is no small feat to shrink\ncontrol electronics into a small package that is compatible with qubit chip fabrication and operation\nconstraints without sacrificing performance. This so-called “wiring challenge” is likely to impact\nthe development of more powerful quantum computers even in the near term. In this paper, we\naddress the wiring challenge of trapped-ion quantum computers. However, implementing workflows is difficult due to the involvement of many black-box tools and the deep infrastructure\nstack necessary for their execution. Simultaneously, user-supporting tools are rare, and the number of available examples is much\nlower than in classical programming languages. We performed three user studies in\ntwo scientific domains to evaluate ChatGPT for comprehending, adapting, and extending workflows. Our results indicate that LLMs\nefficiently interpret workflows but achieve lower performance for exchanging components or purposeful workflow extensions. We\ncharacterize their limitations in these challenging scenarios and suggest future research directions.\nKeywords: Large Language Models, Scientific Workflows, User Support, ChatGPT\n\n1. København K, Denmark\n\nABSTRACT\nSince the creation of the Billboard Hot 100 music chart in 1958, the chart has been a window into\nthe music consumption of Americans. Which songs succeed on the chart is decided by consumption\nvolumes, which can be affected by consumer music taste, and other factors such as advertisement\nbudgets, airplay time, the specifics of ranking algorithms, and more. Since its introduction, the chart has\ndocumented music consumerism through eras of globalization, economic growth, and the emergence of\nnew technologies for music listening. In recent years, musicians and other hitmakers have voiced their\nworry that the music world is changing: Many claim that it is getting harder to make a hit but until now,\nthe claims have not been backed using chart data. Here we show that the dynamics of the Billboard\nHot 100 chart have changed significantly since the chart’s founding in 1958, and in particular in the past\n15 years. Whereas most songs spend less time on the chart now than songs did in the past, we show\nthat top-1 songs have tripled their chart lifetime since the 1960s, the highest-ranked songs maintain\ntheir positions for far longer than previously, and the lowest-ranked songs are replaced more frequently\nthan ever. Binarization, as an effective compression technique,\ncan shrink model weights to just 1 bit, significantly reducing the high demands\non computation and memory. However, current binarization methods struggle to\nnarrow the distribution gap between binarized and full-precision weights, while\nalso overlooking the column deviation in LLM weight distribution. To tackle these\nissues, we propose ARB-LLM, a novel 1-bit post-training quantization (PTQ)\ntechnique tailored for LLMs. To narrow the distribution shift between binarized\nand full-precision weights, we first design an alternating refined binarization (ARB)\nalgorithm to progressively update the binarization parameters, which significantly\nreduces the quantization error. Moreover, considering the pivot role of calibration\ndata and the column deviation in LLM weights, we further extend ARB to ARB-X\nand ARB-RC. In addition, we refine the weight partition strategy with columngroup bitmap (CGB), which further enhance performance. Equipping ARB-X\nand ARB-RC with CGB, we obtain ARB-LLMX and ARB-LLMRC respectively,\nwhich significantly outperform state-of-the-art (SOTA) binarization methods for\nLLMs. As a binary PTQ method, our ARB-LLMRC is the first to surpass FP16\nmodels of the same size. The code and models will be available at https:\n//github.com/ZHITENGLI/ARB-LLM.\n1\n\n\n=== 2411.04734v2.pdf ===\narXiv:2411.04734v2 [hep-th] 24 Dec 2024\n\nDecoherence of quantum superpositions by\nReissner-Nordström black holes\n\nRan Li\nDepartment of Physics, Qufu Normal University, Qufu, Shandong 273165, China\n\nE-mail: liran@qfnu.edu.cn\nAbstract: Recently, it was shown by Danielson-Satishchandran-Wald (DSW) that for the massive\nor charged body in a quantum spatial separated superposition state, the presence of a black hole\ncan decohere the superposition inevitably towards capturing the radiation of soft photons or gravitons [1–3]. In this work, we study the DSW decoherence effect for the static charged body in\nthe Reissner-Nordström black holes. By calculating the decohering rate for this case, it is shown\nthat the superposition is decohered by the low frequency photons that propagate through the black\nhole horizon. For the extremal Reissner-Nordström black hole, the decoherence of quantum superposition is completely suppressed due to the black hole Meissner effect. It is also found that the\ndecoherence effect caused by the Reissner-Nordström black hole is equivalent to that of an ordinary\nmatter system with the same size and charge.\n\n\fContents\n1\n\nIntroduction\n\n1\n\n\n=== 2411.16408v1.pdf ===\nLow-Data Classification of Historical Music\nManuscripts: A Few-Shot Learning Approach\nElona Shatri, Daniel Raymond, György Fazekas\n\narXiv:2411.16408v1 [cs.IR] 25 Nov 2024\n\nCentre for Digital Music\nQueen Mary University of London\nLondon, UK\n{e.shatri, d.k.raymond, george.fazekas}@qmul.ac.uk\n\nAbstract—In this paper, we explore the intersection of technology and cultural preservation by developing a self-supervised\nlearning framework for the classification of musical symbols in\nhistorical manuscripts. Binarization, as an effective compression technique,\ncan shrink model weights to just 1 bit, significantly reducing the high demands\non computation and memory. However, current binarization methods struggle to\nnarrow the distribution gap between binarized and full-precision weights, while\nalso overlooking the column deviation in LLM weight distribution. To tackle these\nissues, we propose ARB-LLM, a novel 1-bit post-training quantization (PTQ)\ntechnique tailored for LLMs. To narrow the distribution shift between binarized\nand full-precision weights, we first design an alternating refined binarization (ARB)\nalgorithm to progressively update the binarization parameters, which significantly\nreduces the quantization error. Moreover, considering the pivot role of calibration\ndata and the column deviation in LLM weights, we further extend ARB to ARB-X\nand ARB-RC. In addition, we refine the weight partition strategy with columngroup bitmap (CGB), which further enhance performance. Equipping ARB-X\nand ARB-RC with CGB, we obtain ARB-LLMX and ARB-LLMRC respectively,\nwhich significantly outperform state-of-the-art (SOTA) binarization methods for\nLLMs. As a binary PTQ method, our ARB-LLMRC is the first to surpass FP16\nmodels of the same size.
